<summary>This is the title page for Richard Bovet's 1684 work, which argues against contemporary skepticism regarding the supernatural. It promises to prove the existence of witches and demons through historical analysis and new eyewitness accounts.</summary>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This is the title page of the work "Pandæmonium," establishing the book's structure and the author's intent to prove the existence of the supernatural through historical examples and contemporary accounts.</meta>

# PANDEMONIUM,
# OR THE
# DEVIL'S CLOISTER.
<note>original: "Pandæmonium," a word coined from Greek meaning "Place of all Demons."</note>
->In Two Parts.<-

---
# PART I.
### Giving a brief Account of,
**I.** The Fall of Angels, the seduction of the human race, and their impious acts before the Flood. <note>The Great Flood of Noah.</note>
**II.** The idolatry of the first ages after the Flood; how the abandonment <note>original: "Defection"</note> of true worship for the worship of the Devil was a major step toward infernal pacts <note>original: "Confederacies"</note>.
**III.** Heathen and idol-priests devoted to magic and divination <note>The practice of seeking knowledge of the future by supernatural means</note>, as general examples of diabolical pacts.
**IV.** Arguments proving the existence of witchcraft, with a rebuttal of the skepticism <note>original: "Incredulity"</note> of those who deny it.
**V.** Propositions concerning witches and witchcraft, the character of a witch, and some considerations regarding the origin of their power.
**VI.** Examples of witchcraft and familiarity with devils among the ancient Druids, Sibyls <note>Ancient pagan prophetesses</note>, Vestal Virgins, and heathen priests.
**VII.** Secret pacts of several Popes and Roman priests with the Devil, etc.

---
# PART II.
### Giving plain evidence concerning apparitions, spirits, and witches; proving their real existence through a choice collection of modern accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note> (never before published).

---
->By *Richard Bovett*, Gentleman.<-

---
->*London*, printed for *Thomas Malthus*, at the sign of the<-
->*Sun* in the *Poultry*. <note>A street in the City of London historically known for its poultry market.</note> 1684.<-

But if those people would only remember with what undeniable <note>original: "irrefragable"</note> reason you have opposed and defeated the legions

# Dedicatory.

*tested stories of witches and apparitions do real service to true religion and sound <note>original: "found"</note> philosophy. They are the most effective and suitable <note>original: "accommodate"</note> means for defeating <note>original: "confounding"</note> infidelity and atheism—even in the judgment of the atheists themselves. These skeptics are as much afraid of the truth of these stories as an ape is of a whip. Therefore, they force themselves with all their might <note>original: "might and main"</note> to disbelieve them, because of the dreadful consequences these stories would have for them.*

I thought myself obliged by an indispensable necessity to contribute everything I could to asserting the reality of spiritual beings <note>original: "Existencies"</note>. Consequently, I also sought to show the advantages such subtle agents <note>meaning spirits or demons</note> have to surprise the unwary and entrap the

### CHAPTER 1.

*Discussing the Fall of the Angels, the Seduction of the Human Race, and the General Sins before the Flood of Noah.*

Since the rebellion <note>original: "defection"</note> of the Fallen Angels from their original righteousness <note>original: "rectitude"; their initial state of moral uprightness and obedience to God</note>, or that state of sublime happiness and glory in which they were placed by the Eternal Almighty <note>original: "Omnipotence"</note>, the tireless <note>original: "indefatigable"</note> enemy of souls has been restless in his attempts to expand his own hellish <note>original: "Infernal"</note> kingdom and to draw human <note>In the 17th century, "humane" was the standard spelling for "human."</note> souls away from that glorious bliss <note>original: "Felicity"</note> from which he and his allied <note>original: "Confederate"</note> angels are forever excluded. To carry out this devilish <note>original: "Diabolical"</note> plan, no fraud or trickery <note>original: "Artifice"</note> has been omitted, no delusions or suggestions neglected, and no temptations or allurements...

<header>The Fall of Our First Parents.</header>

...who kept their station in Heaven were out of the reach of his seductions, and there was no hope of a revolt among those Principalities, Powers, and Thrones <note>These are traditional ranks or orders of angels in celestial hierarchy.</note>. Yet the fallen <note>original: "lapsed"</note> Arch-Angel had a design—which he set himself to manage with all his hellish politics <note>original: "Polities"; referring to his schemes, strategies, or methods of manipulation</note>—against the lord of the newly created world. The Almighty Wisdom had formed him after the Divine Image and placed him in the most pleasant Garden of *Eden*, which God himself planted for the solace and comfort of his newly made subject. All sublunary <note>original: "sublunary"; literally "under the moon," a term used to describe the physical, earthly world which was thought to be subject to change, unlike the eternal heavens.</note> creatures were put under his feet, and his dominion would have been eternal had he adhered to the laws of his Sovereign Creator.

Therefore, the treacherous angel set himself to the conquest and subjection of the human race. He made that very bait of pride and ambition—which had cost him the loss of all his own glory and preeminence in the eternal mansions of happiness—I say, he made that very temptation the snare to entrap our credulous first parents. His goal was to cast fallen man into the disfavor of his Maker and plunge him and his posterity into a state of misery and impotence, creating the possibility of them sharing with the revolted cherubim <note>original: "Cherubims"; a high order of angels; here referring to the angels who rebelled alongside Satan.</note> in all the horrors of an eternal banishment from the happiness of heaven.

# The Fall of Our First Parents.

If we but consider the stinging anguish and tormenting reflections that the aspiring <term>Prince of Darkness</term> <note>A common title for Satan or the Devil.</note> must surely suffer—to see himself stripped of all that majesty, beauty, and brilliance which once sat <note>original: "fate"; likely a transcription of "sate," an archaic past tense of "sit."</note> on the brow of one of the greatest of the mighty angels of heaven, and to be condemned to an eternal banishment from all happiness to the dreadful tortures of the <term>Bottomless Pit</term> <note>A biblical reference to Hell or the abyss (Revelation 9:1).</note>—and, on the other hand, reflecting on the excellent, noble, and happy state of the first *Adam*, newly created and placed in a condition little lower than the heavenly angels, we may well suppose him to look with envy on the happiness of our first parents. Moved by outbursts of a diabolical rage and relentless revenge, he set himself, with all his satanic power, cunning, and malice, to spoil and ruin the envied greatness of the human race, and to bring the masterpiece of the <term>New Creation</term> <note>Referring to the physical world and humanity as they were immediately after being created by God.</note> into a state of misery equal to his own.

The crafty tempter soon found the weakness of our first parents, and he cannot be supposed to have been ignorant of the divine command concerning the <term>Tree of Knowledge</term>. What more plausible suggestion could there be than the achievement of a resemblance to God? You shall be

...wise as gods. But he did not reveal this to the man (whose stronger resolve might perhaps have resisted the argument and discovered the snare); instead, he urged it upon the woman, whose desires and passions were stronger and more easily influenced. Here, therefore, he made his assault and obtained that fatal victory from which we can justly date all the miseries and calamities inflicted on fallen and corrupt humanity because of that sin. This was the source and origin of all evils <note>original: "Fons & Origo Malorum"</note>. It was the only floodgate that let in upon the children of Adam all the impieties and their fatal consequences, under which the world has groaned ever since.

In this way, the <term>Apostate Angel</term> <note>A title for Satan, referring to his rebellion and fall from grace.</note> was the cause of Adam's <term>apostasy</term> <note>The abandonment of one's religious faith or principles; in this case, Adam's disobedience to God's command.</note>. Adam was deceived, as the learned Dr. Browne <note>Sir Thomas Browne (1605–1682), a famous English physician and author known for his deep learning and unique prose style.</note> carefully notes on this occasion:

> They were deceived by one another, and that through the greatest disadvantage of delusion: the stronger by the weaker. Thus, his "superstructure" <note>Browne uses this term to refer to Eve, who was "built" from Adam's rib.</note> was his ruin, and the fertility of his sleep <note>A reference to the deep sleep God brought upon Adam to create Eve from his side.</note> became the cause of death for him. **Vulgar Errors, Chapter 2.**

The <term>Tyrant of Hell</term> <note>Satan.</note>, having obtained this shameful victory over our first parents, resolved to pursue it with all the restless attempts of a malicious fiend.

<page-num>6</page-num>

<header>The Fall of Our First Parents.</header>

Now the trembling pair were banished from the fragrant walks of Paradise, which naturally produced all the sweet comforts and reflections of life. They were turned out into an uncultivated world to work for their bread by the sweat of their brows. Their days were filled with labor and toil, and their nights were tedious and troublesome, filled with anxious and worried thoughts. 

The earth was cursed with thorns and barrenness, which could not be corrected without continual labor. The dire effects of listening to the first temptation have brought down all the curses upon the wretched offspring of humanity, under which they have labored ever since. This opened so large a breach in the fortress of the human soul that the black legions of lust and hell have ever since had easy entrance into the yielding city of the battered heart. 

That fortress—which was originally built by divine power and where the image of Eternal Wisdom was once enthroned—was betrayed by the cunning of an enemy from without and the negligence and treachery of the guards within. It now has the black banner of a proud, <term>insulting</term> <note>original: "Insulting" – In this period, this often meant to physically assault or to triumph over someone with insolent contempt.</note> conqueror raised upon its towers, while the soul is barbarously sacked and robbed of all her wealth and ornaments by the tyrant’s assaulting troops.

That poison which (along with the temptation of the first serpent) entered into the thoughts of deluded *Eve*, has since so diffused its venomous <term>tincture</term> <note>original: "tincture" — a slight trace or characteristic of something; in medicine, a concentrated liquid extract.</note> into the imaginations of her spellbound offspring, that they daily become more susceptible to base and sordid impressions. They are now ready to receive as much of the infernal drug as the <term>Empiric</term> <note>original: "Emperick" — a quack doctor or charlatan who relies on trial and error rather than scientific knowledge.</note> of Hell prescribes for them.

How soon this contagion showed itself is apparent from the sacred story of the Bible. Once their minds had stumbled, they fell from good to bad, and from bad to the worst of all. The parents wrangled and accused one another; the serenity of a peaceful innocence had forsaken them. Passion broke loose and showed its dire effects: their corrupted offspring had this malignancy increased within their decaying bodies. 

The son of *Adam* <note>Referring to Cain.</note> stains himself with the blood of his more righteous brother, and does so even at the altar of God. Here, the hostility of the infernal spirit was visibly revealed; for having now cast down our wretched father from a state of pure innocence, the Devil had instilled into the heart of perverted man an opposition to that Goodness from which he had fallen. This opposition showed itself in its darkest and foulest color in that first barbarous murder. From this point, the pale <term>homicide</term> <note>original: "Homicide" — a person who kills another; here referring to Cain.</note> (sentenced

<page-num>21</page-num>

# Idolatry Introductive to Witchcraft.

The history of the **Jews**—specifically the consideration of their revolts and apostasies <note>original: "apostacies"; the abandonment of religious faith or principles</note> from the covenant of the Almighty—should move us. He performed so many wonders for them and executed such judgments among them; he gave them his righteous law, proclaimed his name before them, sent his prophets to them, and distinguished them from all other nations on Earth with special favors. 

This history should effectively move us to consider the foundation we stand upon, so *that he who thinks he stands may take heed lest he fall* <note>a reference to 1 Corinthians 10:12</note>. As the blessed Apostle said, *if these things have been done in the green tree, we may predict what the dry tree should expect* <note>original: "presage"; a reference to Luke 23:31, implying that if the relatively innocent suffer, the guilty should expect far worse</note>. If the backsliding of the **Jews** cost them so dearly, what can we imagine will happen to apostate Christians?

In a strict sense, **charmers** can be understood as those who use a spell or a specific form of words to employ their familiar spirits to bring whatever creatures they demand at their call. They make venomous creatures lose their poisonous nature <note>original: "disarmed of their noxious quality"</note> for as long as they wish, and cause the most ferocious and wild animals <note>original: "brutes"</note> to become manageable and submissive <note>original: "tractable and couchant"</note>.

->The Augur consults the entrails, and as he finishes his task, he repeats the prophecies to the gods above. <note>original: "Consulit Exta Augur, & absolvens, superis effata recantat."</note><-

---

In ancient times, people turned to these **weather-gaugers** <note>original: "Weather-Gagers"</note> to protect their corn, land, vineyards, and cattle, as well as towns and houses, from storms and tempests. This is mentioned by **Seneca** in the fourth book of his *Natural Questions*. They were assigned to a specific office to watch the weather and warn the people when a storm was approaching. 

Upon receiving such a warning, the people hurried to kill a lamb, a chick, or some other young animal. If they had none of these to offer, they were expected to prick their finger, and that blood was accepted as a sacrifice <note>original: "oblation"</note>; then the storm would either stop or be prevented. This was indeed a strange kind of offering, and one might well conclude along with Seneca that clouds have very little connection <note>original: "affinity"</note> with blood or a pricked finger.

# Priests Given to Divination

A **witch** is commonly understood to be a female agent—or a subject acted upon—who has entered into a covenant with the Devil. In a literal sense, she has sold herself to perform wickedness; her primary dealings <note>original: "negotiation"</note> involve harming her neighbors’ persons or property. They commonly have certain growths like teats or nipples on private parts of their bodies, which their **familiars** <term>supernatural entities or demons, often in animal form, believed to serve a witch</term> often suck. Sometimes in person, and sometimes in a dream or trance, they revel with evil spirits in nightly secret gatherings and consultations. 

Those specifically intended here are the ones commonly called **black witches**. There is, besides, another sort termed **white witches**. These, through a devilish cooperation or perceived "good nature," are meant to break charms and bring relief to those whom the others have afflicted. However, it sometimes happens that one witch or the other dies because of the power of the counter-charm. Both of these are condemned to death by Divine Law, according to *Exodus 22:18* <note>original: "Exod. 22. 18." This biblical verse famously states, "Thou shalt not suffer a witch to live."</note>. The persuasion of such people has sometimes been sought and used to entice young women into "unclean folly" <note>A common early modern euphemism for sexual immorality or illicit behavior.</note>.

What were the *pythons* or *pythonics* <note>original: "Pythones" or "Pythonici"; terms derived from the Greek "Python," referring to people possessed by spirits of divination, like the Oracle of Delphi.</note> who were so much sought after in ancient times? # Priests given to Divination.

Rites were performed in various ways and styles; occasionally approaching people with pleasant and entertaining encounters, and at other times frightening them with violent shapes, gestures, and threats. Plato speaks extensively about these matters in his *Epinomis* <note>original: *Epominede*; a dialogue often attributed to Plato that discusses the nature of the cosmos and minor deities</note>, discussing the power and influence of deceased heroes. The Romans <note>original: *Latins*</note> called these spirits *Lemures* <note>spirits of the restless or malignant dead</note> and household gods, as well as *Manes* <note>souls of deceased ancestors</note>, ghosts, *Genii* <note>original: *Genij*; protective spirits of a person or place</note>, and demons. These entities distributed themselves throughout the air, the earth, and various regions of the world, each distinguished by specific roles and duties to which they dedicated themselves.

They were given many other names, such as fauns, satyrs, nymphs, hamadryads <note>nymphs believed to live in trees</note>, and a great number of others. The learned antiquarian Mr. Camden <note>original: *Cambden*; William Camden (1551–1623), an influential English historian</note>, in his work *Britannia*, records several Roman antiquities found on this island. He mentions various altars dedicated to "the ancestral gods" <note>original Latin: *Diis Manibus*</note> and other "spirits of the place" <note>original Latin: *Loci Genij*</note>, and so forth. Additionally, the Apostle Paul mentions many altars built by the Athenians to these kinds of deities, including one he found inscribed "To the Unknown God."

By this, we see what opinion the Reformed Church of *England* has of their detestable polytheism, or their practice of making a plurality of gods. In this, they act exactly as the Holy Scriptures speak of the workings of the Antichrist, *with all the deceivableness of unrighteousness* <note>2 Thessalonians 2:10</note>, and they teach the very *doctrines of devils* <note>1 Timothy 4:1</note>.

That the origin of this saint and angel worship came from the heathens is plain from *Nicephorus* <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335), a Greek church historian</note>, a very credible author, in his *Church History*, book 15, chapter 28. There, he informs us that one Peter *Gnapheus* <note>Also known as Peter the Fuller, a controversial Bishop of Antioch in the 5th century</note>, a heretical Bishop of *Antioch* in the fifth century (which was before the year 500), was the first who instituted the worship of the Virgin *Mary*, and decreed that her name should be called upon in the public prayers of the church. This is likewise testified by the *Historians of Magdeburg* <note>The *Magdeburg Centuries*, the first comprehensive Protestant history of the Christian church, organized by centuries</note>, Century 5, Chapter 10. This may fairly be reckoned as the first public entrance and establishment of saint worship in general, since it is probable the Blessed *Mary* obtained the honor—

The worshipping of angels had no better foundation, for that practice was anciently introduced by certain heretics, then called *Angelici* <note>The "Angelics," a sect mentioned by early historians for their devotion to angels over Christ</note>, as *St. Augustine* testifies in his work *To Quodvultdeus* <note>original: "Ad Quod vult Deum," a treatise on heresies written around 428 AD</note>, chapter 39. Indeed, these seem to have plagued the Church in the apostles' days, causing that warning from *St. Paul* in Colossians 2:18: "Let no man beguile you in a self-imposed <note>original: "voluntary"</note> humility and worshipping of angels, not clinging to the head," etc. The *Greek Scholiast* <note>A commentator on ancient Greek texts; likely referring to Theophylact or Oecumenius</note> (page 697) interprets this as follows: "There were," he says, "various people who, under the pretense of modesty, forbade others to go to Christ by themselves, claiming that the favor of angels must be sought <note>original: "intreated"</note>...

# Priests given to Divinations.

...to introduce us to God. Similarly, *Theodoret* <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), a prominent theologian and bishop</note> writes on the same scripture passage (page 766): "Using the pretext of humility, they advised people to pray to angels, saying that we could neither see, nor understand, nor reach God directly; therefore, we must win His favor through the mediation of angels."

It is evident that this worship of saints and angels is a piece of revived paganism <note>original: "Paganisme"</note>. For the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>, besides their higher gods <note>original: "Dii Superi"</note>—whom they acknowledged to be gods by nature—had their "demons" <note>From the Greek *daimon*, referring here to spirits or lesser deities that act as intermediaries</note> and their "heroes" (the spirits of brave men who had died). They worshipped these—just as our Roman Catholics <note>original: "Papists"</note> do—not in a simple or absolute way, but as intercessors for them to the higher deities. For this reason, *Tully* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> says in his book *On the Laws* <note>original: "de Legibus"</note>:

> Let the gods be worshipped, both those of the "upper house" who were always considered heavenly <note>original: "Celestial"</note>, and those whose own merits have called them into heaven.

And again he says:

> The rights of the spirits of the dead should be kept sacred; let those who have been given over to death be considered divine <note>original: "Deorum manium jura sancta sunto, hos letho datos, Divos habento"</note>.

By this, he means that the rights of the "ghost-gods" should be kept inviolable, and that they should be worshipped as second-rate deities after death. From this, it is evident how exactly our Roman Catholics have renewed the idolatrous laws of their pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note> ancestors. The pagans attributed the same duties to their demons...

<header>Priests given to Divinations.</header>

These demons are what our Roman Catholics <note>original: "Papists"</note> expect their saints to be: mediators, agents, or representatives for them. As Plato says in his *Synopsis*: "All intercourse between gods and men is performed by demons; they are the carriers of men's prayers to the gods, and they bring back the rewards of devotion to mortals." Likewise, Apuleius <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), a Numidian philosopher and writer</note> says in his *On the God of Socrates*:

> All things are done by the will, power, and authority of the heavenly gods, but through the service, labor, and ministry of demons. <note>original Latin: "Cuncta Cælestium voluntate, Numine, & Authoritate fiunt, sed Demonum Obsequio, operi & Ministerio."</note>

By this, they did not ascribe an absolute power to these spirits, but only an intercessory power.

It is certain that Roman Catholics can no more prove that their setting up and worshipping of images is lawful—and not of pagan origin—than the Israelites could justify setting up their golden calves at Dan and Bethel. Nor can they justify it any more than Solomon and the succeeding kings could justify setting up the altars of Baal, Moloch, and the rest of their abominations. These acts are reckoned among the highest provocations against the Almighty, who always warned them through His prophets against that detestable and crying sin, and denounced the wrath of God against them;

In the year of our redemption 606, Boniface IV, Pope of *Rome*, caused a heathen temple called the *Pantheon*—so named because it was built to honor all the gods—to be dedicated to the Virgin *Mary* and all the saints. He likewise instituted the festival called All Saints' Day, during which the Pope himself must read the service.

Many of the images which the pagans had worshipped were also renamed <note>original: "dipt into the name of," suggesting they were "re-baptized" with Christian identities</note> as saints, and under that identity, they are still worshipped. Upon the whole, then—

They are still worshiping wood and stone <note>original: "stocks, and stones," a common biblical and literary idiom for idols</note> and the work of human hands, *regardless of the names or titles used to dignify and distinguish them.* They are guilty of just as much—indeed, much more—idolatry and blasphemy than their predecessors, the pagan Romans. This is because the ancient Romans did not know the rules of the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelical Rule"</note> and could not be considered such willful, stubborn, and uncorrectable opponents of God’s commands. Therefore, what *Lamech* <note>A descendant of Cain in the Book of Genesis</note> said to his two wives may, with some alteration, be applied in this case: *If the pagan is avenged sevenfold, surely the Papist <note>A derogatory term for a Roman Catholic</note> will be avenged seventy-sevenfold.*

They pay the same adoration to their images as they do to God the Father. They represent Him—sometimes—with the image of an old man with a globe in his hand and a respectable <note>original: "reverend"</note> beard. Sometimes they depict the Trinity just as the pagan poets depicted *Cerberus* <note>The three-headed dog of Greek mythology; the author is criticizing the "Three-Faced" depictions of the Trinity</note>: as an image with three heads and faces, and so on. They walk before these images with a solemn pace and fall to the ground on their knees <note>original: "marrow bones"</note>; they even go on pilgrimages to visit them.

Sometimes, it is argued that some people have come forward and given evidence against themselves; and when brought before magistrates, they have (it may be thought) accused themselves without cause by confessing to be witches and describing various things they did (as they supposed) with the help of the Devil. ## CHAPTER VI.

### Examples of Witchcraft and Familiarity with Devils Among the Ancient Druids, Sibyls, Vestal Virgins, and Heathen Priests.

It is one of the black marks that the *Apostle to the Gentiles* <note>St. Paul the Apostle, known for his mission to spread Christianity to the non-Jewish world.</note> gives us regarding the *Antichrist*: that *he shall sit in the Temple of God and show himself to be worshipped as God*. If we consider the temples, groves, altars, sacrifices, and priests that the *ancients* erected and consecrated to their *infernal deities* in the days of their blindness and mindless idolatry, we will find that this demand for *worship* and *adoration* claimed by the *Antichrist* was, in former ages, paid to the *Devil* himself. Furthermore, we see that the apostate Church of *Rome* seized these sacred rites for its *rebel head* <note>A common 17th-century Protestant critique referring to the Pope.</note> in imitation of the *sacrifices* that their idolatrous ancestors paid to the *rebel* and *apostate angels*.

He specifically relates many of their verses, which are full of superstitions and tradition, having no connection <note>original: "affinity"</note> at all with the holy religion, but instead tending to advance the reputation <note>original: "Credit"</note> of their pagan shrines.

The learned *Wierius* <note>Johann Weyer (1515–1588), a Dutch physician and occultist who was one of the first to argue that those accused of witchcraft were often suffering from mental illness, though he still believed in demons.</note> in his book *On the Deceptions of Demons* <note>original: "de Præstigiis Dæmonum"</note>, Book 1, Chapter 7, considers most of the *Sibyls* <note>original: "Sybils"</note> to be no better than *fanatics* <note>original: "Enthusiastæ"</note> and *spirit-mediums* <note>original: "Pythonistæ"; derived from the "Pythia," the priestess of the Oracle of Delphi who was said to be possessed by a spirit.</note>. He places them among the number of those against whom the mighty prophet *Moses* made a law stating that those who turned to them should be stoned. He is furthermore of the opinion that through their writings, the frenzied <note>original: "Frantick"</note> *Romans* were drawn into many of their ecstatic and superstitious pursuits of their many gods. 

And although some extraordinary prophecies relating to the birth of the glorious *Messiah* are found in some of their writings, these do not excuse the gross *demon-worship* <note>original: "Dæmonolatry"</note> of the rest. This is no more than how the prediction of the *Delphic Oracle* cited before cannot be supposed to atone for the wretched abandonment <note>original: "Derelictions"</note> of the true and *holy God*, caused by the lustful <note>original: "Libidinous"</note> searches of the nations.

4. She is said to be the *Cumaean Sibyl* <note>original: *Sybilla Cumæa*</note>. It is reported that she was one of the *Branchidae* <note>A hereditary clan of priests who served the temple of Apollo</note>, or priests of *Apollo*, who attended an old altar in the *Milesian* fields near the city of *Cuma*. 

When *Pactyas* the *Persian* had fled for refuge to the inhabitants of that city, he was commanded by *Mazares*, their great General, to be delivered into his hands. The people of *Cuma* then consulted their old oracle and were commanded by the Sibyl to deliver him up. 

However, a man named *Aristodicus*, who was a person of note among them, was reluctant to deliver someone who had entrusted the protection of his life into their hands. He delayed leaving the temple and, seeing some nests of young sparrows around the place, was about to carry them away. Suddenly, he heard a voice from the altar, speaking to him like this:

> "O you most wicked of men, what arrogant boldness has so far possessed you that you presume to take away my suppliants <note>People seeking sanctuary or religious protection</note> and those whom I have taken under my protection?"

Upon this, *Aristodicus* returned this bold and frank answer:

> "Do you, O King, help and protect your own suppliants, yet command us to betray the life of *Pactyas* to the *Persians*?"

## CHAPTER 7

### The Conspiracy of several Popes and Roman Priests with the Devil. These were the principal encouragers and promoters of Idolatry in the Church.

Having given an account as succinctly as I could of the idolatry of the Gentiles and the Jews, along with its mischievous consequences in the previous chapters, my approach now leads me to relate in this chapter how fatal the adoration of idols has been—and must still be—to those who continue it, even if under a different name <note>original: "denomination"</note>. ### Popes with the Devil, etc.

To support the authority of the stories <note>original: "Fables"</note> mentioned above, that respected clergyman <note>original: "worthy Divine"</note> quotes their own various authors, citing the specific books and pages where they are told. To confirm this, I must refer you to page 45 of his previously mentioned book. 

And is it not clear to everyone—unless they willfully blind themselves to their own deception—that these stories, if true, are nothing but diabolical tricks <note>original: "Diabolical Cheats"</note>? They are of such a nature that they cannot be imagined to confirm the teachings of the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelical Doctrine"</note>, but rather the superstitions and religious orders imposed upon the world by the Roman Catholics <note>original: "Romanists"</note>. Or, if they are not true, then we may conclude that they are among those *lying wonders* foretold in the Scriptures, by which the Antichrist shall attempt to establish his *blasphemies* among a *gullible* <note>original: "Credulous"</note> and *prejudiced* <note>original: "Bigotted"</note> *generation*. 

And what can their [Church] Fathers, who report those foolish *legends* <note>original: "sottish"</note>, be considered, other than the kind of people identified by the Prophet *Ezekiel* <note>Though the author attributes this to Ezekiel, the quote is actually from Jeremiah 5:31</note> when he says: "*The prophets prophesy lies, and the priests rule by their own authority, and my people love to have it so; but what will you do in the end?*" O depraved and adulterous Church, that imposes on her converts <note>original: "Proselytes"</note>—

A Bishop, overcome with anger toward a convent of *Franciscans*, was resolved to expel them from his city, and to do so the following day. ## Notice <note>original: "Advertisement"</note>

Although in the account <note>original: "Relation"</note> just recited there is no mention made of any suspected witch by whose power the children were brought to that miserable state, and some of the physicians who treated them are of the opinion that there was no witchcraft <term>fascination</term> <note>Historical term for the act of bewitching or casting a harmful spell, often associated with the "evil eye."</note> involved—believing it was purely the effect of a natural illness <term>distemper</term>—I must ask their pardon if I disagree with them for the following reasons:

#### *A remarkable story <note>original: "passage"</note> concerning someone called the* Fairy-Boy *of* Leith *in* Scotland, *given to me by my worthy friend* Captain George Burton, *and certified <note>original: "attested"</note> under his own hand.*

About fifteen years ago, I had business that kept me for some time at *Leith*, which is near *Edinburgh* <note>original: "Eden-borough"</note> in the Kingdom of *Scotland*. I often met some of my acquaintances at a certain house there, where we used to drink a glass of wine for refreshment <note>original: "refection"</note>. The woman who kept the house had an honest reputation among her neighbors, which made me pay more attention to what she told me one day about a *Fairy-Boy* (as they called him) who lived in that town. She had given me such a strange account of him that I told her I wished to see him at the first opportunity, which she promised to arrange. ## The Fourth Report <note>original: "Relation"</note>.

### Giving an account of the Demon <note>original: "Dæmon"</note> of Spreyton <note>original: "Spraiton"</note> in the County of Devon, in the year 1682.

That which was published in May 1683 regarding the demon or demons of Spreyton <note>original: "Spraiton"</note> was an extract from a letter by T. C., Esquire, a nearby neighbor of that place. Although it needed little further confirmation beyond the credibility that the learning and high social status <note>original: "Quality"</note> of that gentleman had given it, much of the story was also known to and reported by the Reverend Minister of Barnstaple, who lives near Spreyton. 

Since I have recently received fresh testimonials confirming the truth <note>original: "veracity"</note> of that report, and because it was originally intended to be included here, I thought it appropriate—given its strangeness—to print it here a second time, exactly as I had transcribed it then.

Around the month of November in the year 1682, in the parish of Spreyton in the county of Devon, one Francis

The first of these apparitions <note>original: "Apparitions"</note> seems to be like that of Mistress Bretton, mentioned in Mr. Glanvill's <note>Joseph Glanvill (1636–1680) was a writer and philosopher who chronicled supernatural events in his work "Saducismus Triumphatus"</note> sixteenth report. ## The Eighth Account <note>original: "Relation"</note>

### *Of various strange appearances of spirits in a nobleman’s house in the West Country.*

Around the year 1667, while staying with some distinguished persons <note>original: "Persons of Honour"</note> at the house of a nobleman in the West Country—a building that had formerly been a *nunnery*—I must admit I had often heard the servants and others who lived or stayed there speak frequently of the noises, disturbances <note>original: "stirs"</note>, and apparitions that often troubled the house. However, at that time, I had no fear of it. Because the house was full of guests <note>original: "Strangers"</note>, the nobleman’s steward, Mr. *C.*, shared a room with me in a fine wood-paneled <term>wainscot</term> room called "*My Lady’s Chamber*." We went to our room quite early, and since there was a good fire burning in the room, we spent some time reading, an activity he greatly enjoyed. Then, after getting into bed and putting out the candles, we noticed that the room was very bright,

## The Ninth Account <note>original: "Relation"</note>

*An account of the appearance <note>original: "Apparition"</note> of fairies, their appearing to hold a fair, and what happened to a certain man who tried to join them.*

Once, while reading the eighteenth story in Mr. Glanvil’s <note>Joseph Glanvil (1636–1680) was an English clergyman and philosopher known for his writings on the supernatural, particularly "Saducismus Triumphatus."</note> *Accounts*, page 203, concerning an Irishman who was nearly carried away by spirits and the banquet they had spread before them in the fields, and so on, it reminded me of a story I had often heard about fairies—or spirits, as the local people call them—who showed themselves in large groups <note>original: "Companies"</note> at various times. Sometimes they would seem to dance, and at other times they appeared to hold a large fair or market. I made it my business to ask the neighbors how much credit should be given to these reports, and many of the local residents confirmed this account to me.

The place where they most frequently showed themselves was on the side of a hill named *Blackdown* <note>a range of hills on the border of Somerset and Devon</note>, between the parishes of *Pitminster* and *Chestonford*, not many miles from *Taunton* <note>original: "Tanton"</note>. Those who have had occasion to travel that way have frequently seen them there, appearing like men and women of a stature generally near the smaller size of men. Their clothing <note>original: "habits"</note> used to be of red, blue, or green, according to the old style of country dress, with high-crowned hats.

One time, about fifty years ago, a person (living at *Combe St. Nicholas*, a parish lying on one side of that hill near *Chard*) was riding toward his home that way and saw just before him on the side of the hill a great company of people. They seemed to him like country folk assembled as if at a fair. There were all sorts of goods, to his appearance, just as at our ordinary fairs: pewterers <note>craftsmen who make or sell pewter metalware</note>, shoemakers, peddlers with all kinds of trinkets, fruit, and drinking booths. He could not remember anything which he had usually seen at fairs but what he saw there. It occurred to him that it might be some fair for *Chestonford*, there being a significant one held at some

He lived in *Combe* <note>Combe St. Nicholas</note> and gave an account of this incident <note>original: "Accident"</note> to anyone who asked for more than twenty years afterward. I received this story <note>original: "Relation"</note> from a person of reputable character <note>original: "Person of known Honour"</note> who heard it from the man himself.

## The Tenth Account <note>original: "Relation"</note>

->*An Account of Two Spirits Which Appeared to Two Servant Maids in the House of Mrs. Aysh of South Petherton, in the Year 1680.*<-

In South *Petherton*, in the county of *Somerset*, lives a gentlewoman who is very well known to all the neighboring gentry. She is respected not only for her ancient family lineage but for her extraordinary piety and even more illustrious charity. I cannot mention her without honorable respect, as I have often had the happiness of being entertained with the most obliging kindness by both the virtuous mother and her children, who share her noble character <note>original: "Congenerous Issue"</note>.

It was on *Midsummer* Day <note>June 24, a traditional time for folklore and supernatural events</note> in the year 1680 that I happened to pay a visit to that worthy family. Finding the lady and her daughters at home, and after exchanging common pleasantries, the eldest of the daughters (who is a very intelligent and accomplished lady) informed me that there had been the strangest thing...

## Notice <note>original: "Advertisement"; in this context, it refers to an author's note or additional commentary.</note>

1. *I have often been told of some people who have fasted on Midsummer Eve <note>The night before the feast of St. John the Baptist, traditionally associated with magic and divination.</note> and then gone into the church porch to see who would die in that parish during the following year. It was said that the spirits of those people would come one after another—in the same order in which they were destined to die—and knock at the church door. I remember I was once told of one of these "watchers" who fell fast asleep, so that no one in the group could wake her. During that deep sleep, the likeness of that very person appeared and knocked at the church door; afterward, when she woke up, she could give no account of anything that had happened, saying only that she had been asleep—until the rest of the group informed her of what they had seen.*

2. *Whether the appearances in the previous story were the spirits of the two young men—who, catching the maids napping at that time of night, might have paid a visit to their sweethearts—or whether they were spirits of another nature that assumed their likeness, I must*

<meta>catchword: leave</meta>

<summary>The author describes the folklore of "church porch watching" on Midsummer Eve to predict deaths and questions whether the visions seen by the maids were the men's actual spirits or deceptive entities.</summary>
<keywords>Midsummer Eve, church porch, death omens, folklore, spirits, divination, parish traditions, supernatural appearances</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of supernatural sightings on Midsummer Eve, specifically debating whether the entities seen were the actual people or deceptive spirits. The previous page ended with the author considering if the apparitions were truly the young men or other spirits taking their form.</meta>

<page-num>217</page-num>
<header>A Collection of Accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note></header>
<sig>L</sig>

leave it to the learned to judge; I must confess I am inclined to believe the latter. It seems to me, based on the rattling of the gate, the noise of the chains, the prancing of the horse, and the terror of the spaniel (which I knew, and he was a sturdy dog)—I say, based on all these circumstances, I would imagine that these spirits were not of as gentle and smooth a temperament as they appeared to be to the maids.

3. Many people doubt what special charm can be attributed to fasting on *Midsummer Eve* <note>The night of June 23rd, traditionally associated with magic and folk rituals.</note>, and the ceremonies that follow it, more than to similar abstinence at any other time. But why might there not be *magical days* and *seasons*, just as there are *planetary hours* <note>In astrology, specific hours of the day believed to be ruled by certain planets, influencing the success of magical or medical actions.</note>? The Devil is called the *Prince of Darkness* because he most commonly shows himself in the depth of the night; conjurers and magicians call upon him most during that season. He has an aversion to the light, as all those who perform evil works do.

There has been much talk about the gathering of *fern seed* (which is considered a *magical herb* <note>original: "Mugical Herb"</note>) on the night of *Midsummer Eve*. ## The Tenth Account <note>original: "Relation"</note>.

### *An account of the death of the most prominent member of a certain family, foretold by rats eating the wall hangings of a room.*

At Kitsford in *Devonshire*, which is now the estate <note>original: "Seat"</note> of *Thomas Wood*, Esquire; I remember very well dining in the parlor there with the lady who was the mother of the gentleman mentioned above. She showed me a large hole in the wall hangings <note>Tapestries or fabric used to cover walls for decoration and insulation.</note> of the room near one of the windows, supposedly eaten by rats. It was almost at the top of the room, and she said this happened only a few weeks before her husband’s death. Sometime later, while dining in the same room again, I saw another hole eaten just below the first one. The lady was pleased to say <note>In this context, she remarked or observed.</note> that it foretold <note>original: "foreshew"</note> her own death. And indeed, a very short time later, she died on a *Sunday* morning, with—

Within a very short time afterward, the silk merchant died while he was returning from a journey to *London*.

## Notice <note>original: "Advertisement"</note>

Rats and toads are both looked upon as harmful <note>original: "noxious"</note> creatures, and are therefore generally loathed by all people. Most have a natural dislike for that sort of pest, except perhaps for witches and those who are said to care for them. And why shouldn't there be magical animals, just as there are magical plants? 

But by what kind of instinct these creatures might foresee such events—or, if they do not foresee them, for what reason they foretell and predict such catastrophes in this manner—is very difficult to determine. They are generally seen as ominous, as are crows, ravens, and screech owls, which often gather at the windows or rooftops of houses where people are dying. Usually, the arrival of these birds at certain houses and locations is followed by a corresponding death <note>original: "Fatality"</note>. 

It is also not unusual for people to have premonitions <note>original: "presages"</note> of their own passing into the next world, which per-

## The Fourteenth Account <note>original: "Relation"</note>

### *An Account of a Person who was Driven to Madness by Carrying a Girdle from one Witch to another.*

Near the River Tweed in **Scotland**, a woman suspected of being a witch had a very sick child. Seeing that she could not help the child through lawful means, she turned to her diabolical arts. Through this magic, she could not free her child from the illness unless she transferred either the same disease, or a worse one, onto another person. Otherwise, she would have gone against the interests of her infernal master <note>The Devil</note>. This was beyond her power to do, even if she had the inclination—which she undoubtedly did not. 

Hearing that a scrivener <term>a professional scribe or clerk</term> was traveling two or three miles to a place where she had an acquaintance who served the same master as she did—namely, the Prince of Darkness—she went to him and asked him to carry a girdle <note>a belt or cord worn around the waist</note> to her friend. Her design

The man took the belt <note>original: "Girdle"</note> from her, and when he arrived at the location, he went and delivered it to the designated person. When he woke up again, he was violently insane <note>original: "raging Mad"</note>, and he continued in that state for a long time afterward without relief. For the rest of his life, he suffered from madness during the three hot months of summer and at the full moon. His son, who was born a considerable time after this, inherited the same illness <term>Distemper: a physical or mental illness or ailment</term>; as far as I know, he is still alive and has the same fits at the usual times, as does one of the son's daughters.

#### *A strange apparition seen by a man as he was going home two miles on a winter night, near Kinneel by the River Forth in Scotland.*

unattended by anyone; which made him easily guess what it was. Besides, it is not usual in that place to bury anyone at night, except for people of the highest social rank <note>original: "Persons of the greatest Quality"</note>. This apparition, as you might imagine, did startle him a little, since there were no houses near him and it was a desolate place <note>original: "wild place"</note>. 

He thought to avoid it by leaving the main road <note>original: "High-way"</note> for some side road; but when he did so, he found it did not help him at all. For at the very time he was turning himself around, the apparition moved from the main road and walked directly in front of him, keeping the same distance as before. When he observed this, he returned to the main road again. He attempted to do this several times but was treated in the same manner as before. 

Consequently, he decided to keep straight on his way without turning to either the right or the left, praying to God to preserve him from the Devil or any of his messengers <note>original: "Emissaries"</note>. The phantom <note>original: "Spectre"</note> stayed a little ahead of him, always maintaining the same distance; so if he walked slowly, it likewise slowed its pace, and if he quickened his steps, it moved faster too. He followed it in this manner,

<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 236, where a man is being followed by a supernatural funeral procession near his home.</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>A Collection of Accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note></header>

until at last it came to a small stone bridge over a brook, about a quarter of a mile from his house. The brook was narrow, but not so narrow that a man could jump over it; in the winter, the water would reach above a man's waist <note>original: "strike a Man above the middle"</note>. The four ghosts that carried this corpse <note>original: "dead Corps"</note>, when they reached this place, laid the coffin across the bridge, so that the man could not cross the bridge unless he stepped over the coffin. 

The man, when he arrived, came to a standstill, not knowing what to do in this situation. He had no desire to wade through the brook, considering the season was cold. He did not dare to cross the bridge and step over it, for he did not know what power the apparition might have to harm him if he risked doing so. 

While he was pondering <note>original: "musing"</note> this, he thought of a strategy <note>original: "Expedient"</note> that he believed would allow him to safely cross the bridge without being hurt. His plan was this: he intended to try to pry <note>original: "prize"</note> it off the bridge and into the water with his cane, for he did not dare to risk touching it with his hands. But when he set about it and pried with all

<page-num>238</page-num>
<header>A Collection of Accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note></header>

his strength, he found it remained as immovable as a rock; yet he continued trying for a considerable time, until at last he broke his cane. Afterward, seeing no possibility of getting across the bridge, he was forced <note>original: "necessitated"</note> to go through the water, despite the coldness of the season. 

When he had reached the other side, he saw the four ghosts take up the coffin again on their shoulders and carry it off the highway. He watched them until they carried it over a little rise <note>original: "Eminence"</note> (a piece of ground higher than the rest, resembling a hill but not as high), but after that, he saw it no more. 

Afterward, he went home to his house, and as soon as he saw the light of the candle that was burning in the house, he immediately fell down upon the ground. (This, people say, is common for persons who are frightened by apparitions.) His wife and servants, seeing what had happened to him, immediately picked him up, revived him <note>original: "brought him to life again"</note>, and asked him what might be the cause of it. He told them that he knew of no cause, since he had found himself in very good health all the day before, unless it were an apparition he saw on the way as he came home, recounting <note>original: "rehearsing"</note> the story as it is related above.

<summary>After failing to move the ghostly coffin with his cane, the man wades through the cold brook; upon arriving home, he collapses from the shock of the encounter and shares his story with his family.</summary>

## Notice <note>original: "Advertisement"</note>

*I have heard this story told by several people of good reputation who lived in the same town as the man, and who heard it directly from his own mouth. I have seen the man myself several times, but I do not remember ever having the opportunity to be in his company—at least not at the time when he told the story mentioned above.*

*Therefore, let no one doubt that there are intelligent beings <note>original: "Intelligencies"</note> in the world besides those wrapped in "garments of clay" <note>referring to human bodies</note>. We see actions beyond the reach of our understanding, performed by beings that seem to be made of air <note>original: "Aerial"</note>, which surpass the strength, power, and ability of even the strongest mortal.*

->THE END <note>original: "FINIS"</note><-
